# 6 batched low rank update

# 6.1 simple low rank update

in this section, we show how to maintain leverage score under low rank update. first, we start with a preconditioning lemma showing that given two spectrally similar matrix, we can invert one matrix faster by knowing the inverse of the other matrix.

lemma 6.1. given  $n \times n$  psd matrices  $a$  and  $m$  such that  $0 \preceq m \preceq a \preceq \kappa \cdot m$ . for any integer  $t \geq 1$  such that  $\kappa (1 - 1 / \kappa)^{t + 1} < 1$ , we have

$$
f (m, t) \preceq a ^ {- 1} \preceq \frac {1}{1 - \kappa (1 - 1 / \kappa) ^ {t + 1}} \cdot f (m, t)
$$

where  $f:\mathbb{r}^{n\times n}\times \mathbb{n}\to \mathbb{r}^{n\times n}$  is defined as

$$
f (m, t) = \frac {1}{\kappa} m ^ {- 1} \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {t} (i - \frac {1}{\kappa} a m ^ {- 1}) ^ {i}.
$$

furthermore, if  $m^{-1}$  and  $a$  are given explicitly, for any  $v \in \mathbb{r}^{n \times r}$ , then we can compute  $f(m, t) \cdot v$  in  $o(\mathcal{t}_{\mathrm{mat}}(n, n, r) \cdot t)$  time.

proof. note that

$$
\begin{array}{l} a ^ {- 1} = m ^ {- \frac {1}{2}} (m ^ {- \frac {1}{2}} a m ^ {- \frac {1}{2}}) ^ {- 1} m ^ {- \frac {1}{2}} \\ = \frac {1}{\kappa} m ^ {- \frac {1}{2}} \left(i - \left(i - \frac {1}{\kappa} m ^ {- \frac {1}{2}} a m ^ {- \frac {1}{2}}\right)\right) ^ {- 1} m ^ {- \frac {1}{2}}. \tag {2} \\ \end{array}
$$

using

$$
0 \preceq i - (1 / \kappa) \cdot m ^ {- \frac {1}{2}} a m ^ {- \frac {1}{2}} \preceq (1 - 1 / \kappa) \cdot i
$$

and

$$
\sum_ {i = 0} ^ {t} x ^ {i} \leq (1 - x) ^ {- 1} \leq \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {t} x ^ {i} + \kappa (1 - 1 / \kappa) ^ {t + 1} \quad \forall x \in [ 0, 1 - 1 / \kappa ],
$$

we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {t} \left(i - \frac {1}{\kappa} m ^ {- \frac {1}{2}} a m ^ {- \frac {1}{2}}\right) ^ {i} \preceq \left(i - \left(i - \frac {1}{\kappa} m ^ {- \frac {1}{2}} a m ^ {- \frac {1}{2}}\right)\right) ^ {- 1} \\ \preceq \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {n} \left(i - \frac {1}{\kappa} m ^ {- \frac {1}{2}} a m ^ {- \frac {1}{2}}\right) ^ {i} + \kappa \cdot (1 - 1 / \kappa) ^ {t + 1} i \tag {3} \\ \end{array}
$$

multiplying  $\frac{1}{\kappa} m^{-1}$  on both sides of the above equation with a formula  $a^{-1}$  (eq. (2)), we get

$$
\frac {1}{\kappa} m ^ {- 1} \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {t} \left(i - \frac {1}{\kappa} m ^ {- \frac {1}{2}} a m ^ {- \frac {1}{2}}\right) ^ {i} \preceq a ^ {- 1} \preceq \frac {1}{\kappa} m ^ {- 1} \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {t} \left(i - \frac {1}{\kappa} m ^ {- \frac {1}{2}} a m ^ {- \frac {1}{2}}\right) ^ {i} + (1 - 1 / \kappa) ^ {t + 1} m ^ {- 1}
$$

using the definition of  $f(m, t)$ , we have that

$$
f (m, t) \preceq a ^ {- 1} \preceq f (m, t) + (1 - 1 / \kappa) ^ {t + 1} m ^ {- 1}.
$$

using  $m^{-1}\preceq \kappa a^{-1}$  , we have

$$
f (m, t) \preceq a ^ {- 1} \preceq f (m, t) + \kappa \cdot (1 - 1 / \kappa) ^ {t + 1} a ^ {- 1}.
$$

the result follows from some rearranging.

![](images/e7f3d8e19cf024cbb497f131720c04a8cedee38bbe093a46de8f40e4aa940476.jpg)

now, we show how to do leverage score update under monotone updates.

lemma 6.2. given a matrix  $a \in \mathbb{r}^{m \times n}$ , non-negative weights  $w, w^{\mathrm{new}} \in \mathbb{r}^m$  such that  $w^{\mathrm{new}} \geq w$  with  $m = o(n)$  and  $\| w^{\mathrm{new}} - w \|_0 = k$ . let  $\beta > 1$  denote the parameter such that  $a^\top w^{\mathrm{new}} a \preceq \beta \cdot a^\top w a$ . given some explicit matrix  $u \in \mathbb{r}^{n \times n}$  and  $u^{\mathrm{new}} \in \mathbb{r}^{n \times n}$  such that

$$
u ^ {- 1} \preceq a ^ {\top} w a \preceq (1 + 1 / \log n) u ^ {- 1},
$$

$$
\left(u ^ {\mathrm {n e w}}\right) ^ {- 1} \preceq a ^ {\top} w ^ {\mathrm {n e w}} a \preceq (1 + 1 / \log n) \left(u ^ {\mathrm {n e w}}\right) ^ {- 1}.
$$

then, for any  $\epsilon \in (0,1 / 2)$ , we can output a vector  $c\in \mathbb{r}^m$  such that

$$
\left\| c - \left(\sigma \left(w ^ {\text {n e w}}\right) - \sigma (w)\right) \right\| _ {2} \leq \epsilon
$$

in time

$$
o (\mathcal {t} _ {\mathrm {m a t}} (n, n, k) \cdot (1 + \log_ {\log (n)} (\beta k / \epsilon))).
$$

the same statement holds for decreasing  $w$ , namely  $w^{\mathrm{new}} \leq w$ , with  $a^\top w^{\mathrm{new}}a \succeq \beta^{-1}a^\top wa$ .

proof. we note that the decreasing case follows from the increasing case by swapping  $w$  and  $w^{\mathrm{new}}$ . hence, we focus on the increasing case. using lemma 6.1, we can construct  $u_{\epsilon}$  such that  $u_{\epsilon}$  is low-degree polynomial of  $u$  and  $a^{\top}wa$  and that

$$
u _ {\epsilon} \preceq (a ^ {\top} w a) ^ {- 1} \preceq (1 + \epsilon) u _ {\epsilon}. \tag {4}
$$

we define  $u_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{new}}$  similarly. we will show how to use  $u_{\epsilon}$  and  $u_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{new}}$  to approximate  $\sigma(w^{\mathrm{new}}) - \sigma(w)$ . first, we note that

$$
\sigma (w ^ {\mathrm {n e w}}) - \sigma (w) = (w ^ {\mathrm {n e w}} - w) \cdot \tau (w ^ {\mathrm {n e w}}) + w \cdot (\tau (w ^ {\mathrm {n e w}}) - \tau (w)).
$$

we define  $c_{i}, c_{1,i}, c_{2,i}$  as follows:

$$
\begin{array}{l} c _ {i} = \sigma (w ^ {\mathrm {n e w}}) _ {i} - \sigma (w) _ {i} \\ = \underbrace {\left(w ^ {\text {n e w}} - w\right) _ {i} \cdot \tau \left(w ^ {\text {n e w}}\right) _ {i}} _ {c _ {1, i}} + \underbrace {w _ {i} \cdot \left(\tau \left(w ^ {\text {n e w}}\right) - \tau (w)\right) _ {i}} _ {c _ {2, i}} \\ \end{array}
$$

we estimate the right hand side by

$$
\widetilde {c} _ {i} := \underbrace {\left(w ^ {\text {n e w}} - w\right) _ {i} \cdot \left(a u _ {\widetilde {\epsilon}} ^ {\text {n e w}} a ^ {\top}\right) _ {i , i}} _ {\widetilde {c} _ {1, i}} + \underbrace {w _ {i} \cdot \left(a \left(\left(u _ {\widetilde {\epsilon}} ^ {- 1} + \delta\right) ^ {- 1} - u _ {\widetilde {\epsilon}}\right) a ^ {\top}\right) _ {i , i}} _ {\widetilde {c} _ {2, i}} \tag {5}
$$

where  $\delta = a^{\top}w^{\mathrm{new}}a - a^{\top}wa$  and  $\widetilde{\epsilon} = \frac{\epsilon}{3\beta\sqrt{k}}$ .

cost of computing  $\widetilde{c}$  for the first term in (5). we note that  $w^{\mathrm{new}} - w$  has only  $k$  non-zeros. hence, we only need to compute  $au_{\widetilde{\epsilon}}^{\mathrm{new}}a^{\top}$  on  $k$  of the diagonals. let  $a_s \in \mathbb{r}^{k \times n}$  where each rows of  $a$  that  $w^{\mathrm{new}} \neq w$ . then, we can compute the  $k$  of the diagonals via the whole matrix

$$
a _ {s} u _ {\widetilde {\epsilon}} ^ {\text {n e w}} a _ {s} ^ {\top}.
$$

lemma 6.1 shows that  $u_{\widetilde{\epsilon}}^{\mathrm{new}}$  has  $o(1 + \frac{\log\widetilde{\epsilon}}{\log\log(n)})$  terms and that it takes

$$
o \left(\mathcal {t} _ {\operatorname {m a t}} (n, n, k) \cdot \left(1 + \frac {\log \widetilde {\epsilon}}{\log \log (n)}\right)\right)
$$

to compute  $u_{\tilde{\epsilon}}^{\mathrm{new}}a_s^\top$ . finally, it takes extra  $\mathcal{t}_{\mathrm{mat}}(k,n,k) = o(\mathcal{t}_{\mathrm{mat}}(n,n,k))$  to do the left multiplication on  $a_{s}$ .

for the second term in (5). woodbury matrix identity shows that

$$
\left(u _ {\tilde {\epsilon}} ^ {- 1} + \delta\right) ^ {- 1} - u _ {\tilde {\epsilon}} = u _ {\tilde {\epsilon}} a _ {s} ^ {\top} \left(\delta_ {w} ^ {- 1} + a _ {s} u _ {\tilde {\epsilon}} a _ {s} ^ {\top}\right) ^ {- 1} a _ {s} u _ {\tilde {\epsilon}}
$$

where  $\delta_w \in \mathbb{r}^{k \times k}$  be  $w^{\mathrm{new}} - w$  restricted on non-zeros. by same argument above, we can compute  $a_s u_{\widetilde{\epsilon}} a_s^\top$  in

$$
o \left(\mathcal {t} _ {\mathrm {m a t}} (n, n, k) \cdot \left(1 + \frac {\log \widetilde {\epsilon}}{\log \log (n)}\right)\right)
$$

time.

then, we can compute  $(\delta_w^{-1} + a_s u_{\tilde{\epsilon}} a_s^\top)^{-1}$  in  $\mathcal{t}_{\mathrm{mat}}(k,k,k) = o(\mathcal{t}_{\mathrm{mat}}(n,n,k))$  time. then, we compute

$$
a u _ {\tilde {\epsilon}} a _ {s} ^ {\top} \left(\delta_ {w} ^ {- 1} + a _ {s} u _ {\tilde {\epsilon}} a _ {s} ^ {\top}\right) ^ {- 1} \quad \text {i n t i m e} \mathcal {t} _ {\mathrm {m a t}} (m, n, k) = o \left(\mathcal {t} _ {\mathrm {m a t}} (n, n, k)\right)
$$

and

$$
a _ {s} u _ {\widetilde {\epsilon}} = \left(u _ {\widetilde {\epsilon}} a _ {s} ^ {\top}\right) ^ {\top} \qquad \text {i n t i m e} o \left(\mathcal {t} _ {\mathrm {m a t}} (n, n, k) \cdot \left(1 + \frac {\log \widetilde {\epsilon}}{\log \log (n)}\right)\right).
$$

now, we multiple the two terms above together in time  $\mathcal{t}_{\mathrm{mat}}(n,k,n) = o(\mathcal{t}_{\mathrm{mat}}(n,n,k))$ . hence, the total time is

$$
o \left(\mathcal {t} _ {\mathrm {m a t}} (n, n, k) \cdot \big (1 + \frac {\log \widetilde {\epsilon}}{\log \log (n)} \big)\right) = o (\mathcal {t} _ {\mathrm {m a t}} (n, n, k) (1 + \log_ {\log (n)} (\beta k / \epsilon))).
$$

the accuracy of  $\widetilde{c}$  for the first term in  $\widetilde{c}$ , we have

$$
a (a ^ {\top} w ^ {\text {n e w}} a) ^ {- 1} a ^ {\top} \preceq a u _ {\widetilde {\epsilon}} ^ {\text {n e w}} a ^ {\top} \preceq (1 + \widetilde {\epsilon}) \cdot a (a ^ {\top} w ^ {\text {n e w}} a) ^ {- 1} a ^ {\top}.
$$

hence, we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} | \widetilde {c} _ {1, i} - c _ {1, i} | = \left| (w ^ {\mathrm {n e w}} - w) _ {i} \left(a u _ {\widetilde {\epsilon}} ^ {\mathrm {n e w}} a ^ {\top} - a (a ^ {\top} w ^ {\mathrm {n e w}} a) ^ {- 1} a ^ {\top}\right) _ {i, i} \right| \\ \leq \widetilde {\epsilon} \cdot \left| w _ {i} ^ {\text {n e w}} - w _ {i} \right| \cdot \left(a \left(a ^ {\top} w ^ {\text {n e w}} a\right) ^ {- 1} a ^ {\top}\right) _ {i, i} \\ \leq \widetilde {\epsilon} \cdot w _ {i} ^ {\text {n e w}} \cdot \left(a \left(a ^ {\top} w ^ {\text {n e w}} a\right) ^ {- 1} a ^ {\top}\right) _ {i, i} \\ \leq \widetilde {\epsilon} \\ \end{array}
$$

where we used that  $w^{\mathrm{new}} \geq w$  in the second inequality and leverage score is upper bounded by 1 in the last equality. hence the  $\ell_{\infty}$  norm error of the first term is given by  $\widetilde{\epsilon}$ . hence, the  $\ell_{2}$  norm error is given by

$$
\left\| \widetilde {c} _ {1} - c _ {1} \right\| _ {2} \leq \sqrt {k} \cdot \widetilde {\epsilon}. \tag {6}
$$

for the second term in  $\widetilde{c}$ , we have that the error is given by

$$
\widetilde {c} _ {2, i} - c _ {2, i} = w _ {i} \cdot (a ((u _ {\widetilde {\epsilon}} ^ {- 1} + \delta) ^ {- 1} - u _ {\widetilde {\epsilon}}) a ^ {\top}) _ {i, i} - w _ {i} \cdot (a ((m + \delta) ^ {- 1} - m ^ {- 1}) a ^ {\top}) _ {i, i}
$$

where  $m = a^{\top}wa$ . to simplify the notation, we define  $m_{t,s} = m + t\cdot \delta_2 + s\cdot \delta$  where  $\delta_2 = u_{\tilde{\epsilon}}^{-1} - m$ . then, the error term becomes

$$
(\sqrt {w} a \left(m _ {1, 1} ^ {- 1} - m _ {1, 0} ^ {- 1} - m _ {0, 1} ^ {- 1} + m _ {0, 0} ^ {- 1}\right) a ^ {\top} \sqrt {w}) _ {i, i} \tag {7}
$$

note that

$$
\begin{array}{l} m _ {1, 1} ^ {- 1} - m _ {1, 0} ^ {- 1} - m _ {0, 1} ^ {- 1} + m _ {0, 0} ^ {- 1} \\ = \int_ {0} ^ {1} \int_ {0} ^ {1} \frac {\mathrm {d} ^ {2}}{\mathrm {d} t \mathrm {d} s} m _ {t, s} ^ {- 1} \mathrm {d} t \mathrm {d} s \\ = \int_ {0} ^ {1} \int_ {0} ^ {1} m _ {t, s} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {2} m _ {t, s} ^ {- 1} \delta m _ {t, s} ^ {- 1} + m _ {t, s} ^ {- 1} \delta m _ {t, s} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {2} m _ {t, s} ^ {- 1} d t d s \tag {8} \\ \end{array}
$$

combining (7) and (8), we have that the  $\ell_2$  norm error for the second term is bounded by

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| \widetilde {c} _ {2} - c _ {2} \| _ {2} \leq \left\| \int_ {0} ^ {1} \int_ {0} ^ {1} \sqrt {w} a \left(m _ {t, s} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {2} m _ {t, s} ^ {- 1} \delta m _ {t, s} ^ {- 1} + m _ {t, s} ^ {- 1} \delta m _ {t, s} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {2} m _ {t, s} ^ {- 1}\right) a ^ {\top} \sqrt {w} \mathrm {d} t \mathrm {d} s \right\| _ {f} \\ \leq 2 \max _ {t \in [ 0, 1 ], s \in [ 0, 1 ]} \left\| \sqrt {w} a m _ {t, s} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {2} m _ {t, s} ^ {- 1} \delta m _ {t, s} ^ {- 1} a ^ {\top} \sqrt {w} \right\| _ {f}. \\ \end{array}
$$

to bound the frobenius norm, we note that  $\delta \succeq 0$  by the assumption and that  $\delta_2 = u_{\widetilde{\epsilon}}^{-1} - m\succeq 0$  (4). hence, we have that  $m_{t,s}\succeq m$  and

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| \sqrt {w} a m _ {t, s} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {2} m _ {t, s} ^ {- 1} \delta m _ {t, s} ^ {- 1} a ^ {\top} \sqrt {w} \| _ {f} ^ {2} \\ = \operatorname {t r} \left[ a ^ {\top} w a m _ {t, s} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {2} m _ {t, s} ^ {- 1} \delta m _ {t, s} ^ {- 1} a ^ {\top} w a m _ {t, s} ^ {- 1} \delta m _ {t, s} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {2} m _ {t, s} ^ {- 1} \right] \\ = \operatorname {t r} \left[ m ^ {\frac {1}{2}} m _ {t, s} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {2} m _ {t, s} ^ {- 1} \delta m _ {t, s} ^ {- 1} m m _ {t, s} ^ {- 1} \delta m _ {t, s} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {2} m _ {t, s} ^ {- 1} m ^ {\frac {1}{2}} \right] \\ \leq \operatorname {t r} \left[ m ^ {\frac {1}{2}} m _ {t, s} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {2} m _ {t, s} ^ {- 1} \delta m _ {t, s} ^ {- 1} \delta m _ {t, s} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {2} m _ {t, s} ^ {- 1} m ^ {\frac {1}{2}} \right] \\ = \operatorname {t r} \left[ m _ {t, s} ^ {- \frac {1}{2}} \delta m _ {t, s} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {2} m _ {t, s} ^ {- 1} m ^ {\frac {1}{2}} m ^ {\frac {1}{2}} m _ {t, s} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {2} m _ {t, s} ^ {- 1} \delta m _ {t, s} ^ {- \frac {1}{2}} \right] \\ \leq \operatorname {t r} \left[ m _ {t, s} ^ {- \frac {1}{2}} \delta m _ {t, s} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {2} m ^ {- 1} \delta_ {2} m _ {t, s} ^ {- 1} \delta m _ {t, s} ^ {- \frac {1}{2}} \right]. \\ \end{array}
$$

(4) shows that  $\delta_2 \preceq \widetilde{\epsilon} \cdot m$  and hence  $(m^{-\frac{1}{2}}\delta_2m^{-\frac{1}{2}})^2 \preceq \widetilde{\epsilon}^2 \cdot i$ . continuing the last equation, we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| \sqrt {w} a m _ {t, s} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {2} m _ {t, s} ^ {- 1} \delta m _ {t, s} ^ {- 1} a ^ {\top} \sqrt {w} \| _ {f} ^ {2} \leq \tilde {\epsilon} ^ {2} \cdot \operatorname {t r} \left[ m _ {t, s} ^ {- \frac {1}{2}} \delta m _ {t, s} ^ {- 1} m m _ {t, s} ^ {- 1} \delta m _ {t, s} ^ {- \frac {1}{2}} \right] \\ \leq \widetilde {\epsilon} ^ {2} \cdot \operatorname {t r} \left[ m _ {t, s} ^ {- \frac {1}{2}} \delta m _ {t, s} ^ {- 1} \delta m _ {t, s} ^ {- \frac {1}{2}} \right] \\ \leq \tilde {\epsilon} ^ {2} \cdot \| m ^ {- \frac {1}{2}} \delta m ^ {- \frac {1}{2}} \| _ {f} ^ {2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

finally, using  $\delta$  has rank  $k$ , we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \widetilde {c} _ {2} - c _ {2} \right\| _ {2} \leq 2 \widetilde {\epsilon} \cdot \left\| m ^ {- \frac {1}{2}} \delta m ^ {- \frac {1}{2}} \right\| _ {f} \\ \leq 2 \widetilde {\epsilon} \cdot \sqrt {k} \| m ^ {- \frac {1}{2}} \delta m ^ {- \frac {1}{2}} \| \\ \leq 2 \widetilde {\epsilon} \cdot \beta \sqrt {k}. \tag {9} \\ \end{array}
$$

combining (6) and (9), the total  $\ell_2$  error is bounded by  $2\widetilde{\epsilon}\beta \sqrt{k} +\sqrt{k}\cdot \widetilde{\epsilon}\leq 3\widetilde{\epsilon}\beta \sqrt{k}$ . this explains the choice of  $\widetilde{\epsilon}$ .

# 6.2 batched low rank update

when we are given a sequence of update and we need to compute the change of leverage score, we cannot apply lemma 6.2 directly for the following reasons:

- the update in general involves both increasing some weight and decreasing some weight. this can be fixed by splitting the update into positive update and negative update. (see step 1 in algorithm 2)  
- to get error  $\frac{1}{n^{o(1)}}$ , lemma 6.2 takes at least  $n^2 \log(n)$  time. hence, it is too costly to do a rank 1 update, which can happen if we have alternating positive and negative updates (since we can only apply lemma 6.2 for a monotone change). this can be fixed by moving all positive/update and insert in the front of the update sequence. (see step 2 in algorithm 2)  
- our  $\ell_2$  update are generally dense. this can be fixed by ignoring all small multiplicative update. (see line 44 and 49 of step 3 in algorithm 2)  
- in general, the matrices  $a^\top wa$  can change by a factor of  $e^{\theta (t)}$  after  $t$  iterations. so, lemma 6.2 takes at least  $\omega (\mathcal{t}_{\mathrm{mat}}(n,n,k)\frac{t}{\log\log(n)})$  time. this forces us to do rank  $k$  changes where  $\mathcal{t}_{\mathrm{mat}}(n,n,k)\leq n^2\log \log (n)$ . hence, for  $\ell_2$  update, we need to handle coordinates with not-so-small multiplicative updates separately. this can be fixed by cutting the update sequence into phases of length  $l$  and noticing there are not too many coordinate that is changed by  $e^{\theta (l)}$  factor. (see the rest of step 3 in algorithm 2)

given the above intuitions, theorem 6.3 follows from multiple use of lemma 6.2.

theorem 6.3 (batched update of leverage score). given an initial matrix  $a \in \mathbb{r}^{m \times n}$ , initial weight  $w \in \mathbb{r}_+^m$  and a sequence of  $t$  updates that belong to one of the following forms:

- update: update the weight to  $w$  
- insert: insert a row  $a \in \mathbb{r}^n$  to the matrix  $a \in \mathbb{r}^{m \times n}$  and weight  $w_a$  to the vector  $w$  
- delete: delete a row  $a \in \mathbb{r}^n$  from the matrix  $a \in \mathbb{r}^{m \times n}$  and weight  $w_a$  from the vector  $w$ .

let the sequence of matrices and vectors be  $a^{(0)}, a^{(1)}, \dots, a^{(t)} \in \mathbb{r}^{m \times n}$  and  $w^{(0)}, w^{(1)}, \dots, w^{(t)} \in \mathbb{r}^m$ . we assume that  $t \leq n^{0.08}$ , the maximum number of rows is bounded by  $o(n)$ , and that the changes satisfy

- update:  $\| \log w^{(k)} - \log w^{(k - 1)}\| _2\leq 0.01$  
- insert/delete:  $w_{a}aa^{\top}\preceq 0.01\cdot a^{(k - 1)}w^{(k - 1)}a^{(k - 1)}$

then, there is an algorithm (batchedupdate in algorithm 2 and 3) that finds a vector  $v \in \mathbb{r}^n$  and a vector  $c \in \mathbb{r}^n$  such that

$$
\left\| \log v - \log w ^ {(t)} \right\| _ {\infty} \leq t \cdot n ^ {- 0. 0 8}, \left\| \log v - \log w ^ {(t)} \right\| _ {2} \leq 0. 0 1 \cdot t
$$

and that

$$
\left\| c - \left(\sigma_ {a ^ {(t)}} (v) - \sigma_ {a ^ {(0)}} \left(w ^ {(0)}\right)\right) \right\| _ {2} \leq 1 / n ^ {1 0 0}.
$$

in time  $o(n^{2}t)$

proof.

correctness note that the algorithm 2 involves reducing the sequence  $\{m^{(0,k)}\}$  to  $\{m^{(1,k)}\}$  to  $\{m^{(2,k)}\}$  and finally to  $\{m^{(3,k)}\}$ . all steps maintain the matrix at  $k = 0$ . the first two steps maintain the matrix at the last step. for the last step, we only ignore the rows with multiplicative changes less than  $\eta = n^{-0.08}$ . since there are at most  $t / l = n^{0.08} / \log^3 (n)$  phases, the total accumulated multiplicative changes for one row is  $tn^{-0.08}$ . therefore, we have

$$
m ^ {(3, \mathrm {e n d})} = (a ^ {(t)}) ^ {\top} v a ^ {(t)}
$$

for some  $v$  such that  $\| \log v - \log w^{(t)}\|_{\infty} \leq tn^{-0.08}$ . furthermore, we output the good enough approximation of  $\sigma_{a(t)}(v) - \sigma_{a^{(0)}}(w^{(0)}) = \sigma (m^{(3,\mathrm{end})}) - \sigma (m^{(3,0)})$  where we abused the notation to use  $\sigma (m)$  to denote the leverage score of the rows inside  $m$ . this is simply because we split the difference into

$$
\sigma (m ^ {(3, \mathrm {e n d})}) - \sigma (m ^ {(3, 0)}) = \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {t} \sigma (m ^ {(3, k)}) - \sigma (m ^ {(3, k - 1)})
$$

and estimate each step with  $\ell_2$  error  $n^{-1000}$ . there are at most  $8t$  many terms, so the total error is less than  $n^{-100}$ . (the number of terms can increase by at most a factor of 8 due to step 1, step 2 and step 4.)

ratio on  $m$  within each phase by the assumptions on insert/delete and update, we see that  $0.8m^{(0,k - 1)}\preceq m^{(0,k)}\preceq 1.2m^{(0,k - 1)}$  for all  $k$ . the first step clearly maintains this relation. we claim that the second step also maintains this relation. to see this, we let  $\delta^{(1,k)} = m^{(1,k + 1)} - m^{(1,k)}$ . by the relation, we have that

$$
- 0. 2 m ^ {(1, k)} \preceq \delta^ {(k)} \preceq 0. 2 m ^ {(1, k)}. \tag {10}
$$

note that step 2 simply permutes  $\delta^{(1,k)}$ , namely, for each  $k$  there is an unique  $k'$  such that  $\delta^{(1,k)} = \delta^{(2,k')}$ . finally, we note that  $m^{(1,k)} \preceq m^{(2,k')}$  for all  $k$  because  $m^{(2,k')}$  is simply  $m^{(2,k')}$  plus some

psd matrix (which due to some insert/positive update move into the sum or some delete/negative update removed from the sum). hence, we still have the relation (10).

for the step 3, we should not expect the relation (10). however, we claim that within each phase, let  $m^{(3,k)}$  and  $m^{(3,\overline{k})}$  be two matrices in the phase. then, we have that

$$
2 ^ {- o (l)} m ^ {(3, \bar {k})} \preceq m ^ {(3, k)} \preceq 2 ^ {o (l)} m ^ {(3, \bar {k})}. \tag {11}
$$

the proof for this is same for the phase with increasing  $m$  and the phase with decreasing phase. hence, we only discuss the first case. say  $k_{1}$  is the first matrix in the phase and  $k_{2}$  is the last matrix in the phase. note that the step 3 does not change the matrix  $m^{(3,k_1)}$  and  $m^{(3,k_2)}$  because we only swapping operation order within a phase. hence by (10), we have

$$
m ^ {(3, k _ {2})} = m ^ {(2, k _ {2})} \preceq 2 ^ {o (l)} m ^ {(2, k _ {1})} = 2 ^ {o (l)} m ^ {(3, k _ {1})}.
$$

since all matrix in the phase is sandwiched between  $m^{(3,k_1)}$  and  $m^{(3,k_2)}$  (due to the monotonicity of the sequence of  $m$ ). hence, we have (11).

cost of insert/delete (line 65) now, we bound the runtime. we will show the each phase takes  $o(n^{2}l)$  time. since there are  $o(t / l)$  phases, this shows that the total cost is  $o(n^{2}t)$ .

each phase contains at most  $l$  insert or delete. hence, line 65 involves estimating leverage score up to rank  $l$  update. by (11), we know the matrix is changed by a  $2^{o(l)}$  factor. theorem 6.3 shows the cost is

$$
o \left(\mathcal {t} _ {\operatorname {m a t}} (n, n, k) \left(1 + \log_ {\log (n)} \left(\beta k / \epsilon\right)\right)\right)
$$

where  $\epsilon = n^{-1000}$ ,  $\beta = 2^{o(l)}$  and  $k = o(l)$ . by the choice of  $l = \log^3 (n)$ , we have  $\mathcal{t}_{\mathrm{mat}}(n,n,k) = n^2$  and hence the cost is  $o(n^{2}l)$ .

cost of large update (line 63) each phase contains at most  $l$  updates. by the assumption, each update changes the  $\ell_2$  norm of  $\log w$  by at most  $o(1)$ . note that this is maintained during all steps. hence, after merging all updates into 1, we have  $\| \log w^{(k)} - \log w^{(k-1)} \|_2 = o(l)$ . by the construction of  $w^{(k-\frac{1}{2})}$ , we have that  $\| \log w^{(k)} - \log w^{(k-\frac{1}{2})} \|_2 = o(l)$  and that  $|\log w_i^{(k-\frac{1}{2})} - \log w_i^{(k)}|$  is either 0 or at least log 2 for all  $i$ . hence the number of coordinates that is non-zero in  $\log w^{(k)} - \log w^{(k-\frac{1}{2})}$  is at most  $o(l^2)$ . again, by (11), we know the matrix is changed by a  $2^{o(l)}$  factor. hence, theorem 6.3 shows the cost is

$$
o \left(\mathcal {t} _ {\operatorname {m a t}} (n, n, k) \left(1 + \log_ {\log (n)} \left(\beta k / \epsilon\right)\right)\right)
$$

where  $\epsilon = n^{-1000}$ ,  $\beta = 2^{o(l)}$  and  $k = o(l^2)$ . by the choice of  $l = \log^3 (n)$ , we have  $\mathcal{t}_{\mathrm{mat}}(n,n,k) = n^2$  and hence the cost is  $o(n^{2}l)$ .

cost of small update (line 62) similar to above, we have that  $\| \log w^{(k - \frac{1}{2})} - \log w^{(k - 1)}\| _2 = o(l)$ . furthermore, we have that  $\| \log w^{(k - \frac{1}{2})} - \log w^{(k - 1)}\|_{\infty} = o(1)$ . due to line 44 and 49, we removed all rows with multiplicative changes less than  $\eta$ . hence, the number of coordinates that is non-zero in  $\log w^{(k - \frac{1}{2})} - \log w^{(k - 1)}$  is at most  $o(l^2 /\eta^2)$ . hence, theorem 6.3 shows the cost is

$$
o \left(\mathcal {t} _ {\operatorname {m a t}} (n, n, k) \left(1 + \log_ {\log (n)} (\beta k / \epsilon)\right)\right)
$$

where  $\epsilon = n^{-1000}$ ,  $\beta = o(1)$  and  $k = o(l^2 /\eta^2)$ . by the choice of  $l = \log^3 (n)$  and  $\eta = n^{-0.08}$ , we have  $\mathcal{t}_{\mathrm{mat}}(n,n,k) = n^{2}\log^{2}(n)$  and hence the cost is  $o(n^{2}\log^{3}(n)) = o(n^{2}l)$ .

summary for the cost of the rest of the algorithm, we note that step 1, 2 and 3 simply involves rearrangements and it takes  $o(nt)$  for all of them, much faster than  $o(n^{2}t)$ . finally, we note that theorem 6.3 requires having an approximate explicit matrix inverse. this can be done by matrix maintenance (theorem b.4) which takes  $n^2 l$  per phase. to see this, we note that theorem b.4 takes  $o(n^{2})$  time per rank 1 update and it takes  $o(n^{2})$  time per unit multiplicative  $\ell_{2}$  changes in the weight (if we pick  $\epsilon = 0.01$  in theorem b.4). hence, each phase, the cost is just  $o(n^{2}l)$ .

algorithm 2 batched update of leverage score (theorem 6.3)  
1: procedure batchupdate(\{a $^{(k)} \in \mathbb{r}^{m(k) \times n}, w^{(k)} \in \mathbb{r}^{m(k)}\}_{k=0}$   
2:  
3:  $t_0 \gets t$  and  $m^{(0,k)} \gets a^{(k)\top}w^{(k)}a^{(k)}$  ▷ easier to program via the  $n \times n$  matrices  $a^{\top}wa$   
4:  
5: ▷ step 1: split the update into positive and negative update  
6:  $m^{(1,0)} \gets m^{(0,0)}$   
7: for  $k = 1, \dots, t_0$  do  
8: if  $\text{rank}(m^{(0,k)} - m^{(0,k-1)}) = 1$  then ▷ insert/delete step  
9:  $m^{(1,\text{end} + 1)} \gets m^{(0,k)}$  ▷ append  $m^{(0,k)}$  to the end of the list of matrix  $\{m^{(0,:)}\}$   
10: else ▷ update step  
11: write  $m^{(0,k)} = a^{\top}w^{(k)}a$  and  $m^{(0,k-1)} = a^{\top}w^{(k-1)}a$   
12:  $m^{(1,\text{end} + 1)} \gets a^{\top}\max(w^{(k)}, w^{(k-1)})a$  ▷ positive update:  $m^{(1,\text{end} + 1)} \succeq m^{(1,\text{end})}$   
13:  $m^{(1,\text{end} + 1)} \gets m^{(0,k)}$  ▷ negative update:  $m^{(1,\text{end} + 1)} \preceq m^{(1,\text{end})}$   
14: end if  
15: end  $\leftarrow$  end + 1  
16: end for  
17:  
18: ▷ step 2: move insert / positive update into front  
19:  $m^{(2,0)} \gets m^{(1,0)}, l \gets \log^3(n)$  ▷ l is the size of the phase  
20: for  $k = 1, \dots, t_1$  where  $m^{(1,t_1)}$  is the last element in  $\{m^{(1,:)}\}$  do  
21: if  $m^{(1,k)} \succeq m^{(1,k-1)}$  then ▷ insert / positive update step  
22:  $m^{(2,\text{end} + 1)} \gets m^{(2,\text{end})} + m^{(1,k)} - m^{(1,k-1)}$  ▷ put it into front  
23: end  $\leftarrow$  end + 1  
24: end if  
25: end for  
26:  
27: append enough  $m^{(2,\text{end})}$  into  $\{m^{(2,:)}\}$  such that the last index is a multiple of  $l$   
28: for  $k = 1, \dots, t_1$  do  
29: if  $m^{(1,k)} \prec m^{(1,k-1)}$  then ▷ delete / negative update step  
30:  $m^{(2,\text{end} + 1)} \gets m^{(2,\text{end})} + m^{(1,k)} - m^{(1,k-1)}$  ▷ put it into end  
31: end  $\leftarrow$  end + 1  
32: end if  
33: end for  
34: append enough  $m^{(2,\text{end})}$  into  $\{m^{(2,:)}\}$  such that the last index is a multiple of  $l$   
35:

```txt
algorithm 3 batched update of leverage score (theorem 6.3, continuation of algorithm 2)  
36: ▷ step 3: within each phase, merge rank-1 update together and sparsify non rank-1 update  
37:  $m^{(3,0)} \gets m^{(2,0)}$ ,  $\eta \gets n^{-0.08}$  ▷ ignore all updates with multiplicative change less than  $\eta$   
38: for  $\ell = 1, \dots, t_2 / l$  do ▷ due to line 27 and 34, each phase is monotone  
39: if phase  $\ell$  involves only insert / positive update then  
40:  $\delta_{\text{insert}} \gets \sum_{k} (m^{(2,k)} - m^{(2,k-1)})$  where it sums rank 1 update in phase  $\ell$   
41:  $\delta_{\text{posupdate}} \gets \sum_{k} (m^{(2,k)} - m^{(2,k-1)})$  where it sums rank  $>1$  update in phase  $\ell$   
42:  $m^{(3,\text{end}+1)} \gets m^{(3,\text{end})} + \delta_{\text{insert}}$   
43: write  $m^{(3,\text{end})} = a^{\top}wa$  and  $\delta_{\text{posupdate}} = a^{\top}diag(\delta)a$   
44:  $m^{(3,\text{end}+1)} \gets m^{(3,\text{end})} + a^{\top}diag(\delta_{\text{sparse}})a$  where  $\delta_{\text{sparse}} = \delta 1_{|\delta| \geq \eta |w|}$ .  
45: else  
46:  $i \gets$  the list of rows deleted in phase  $\ell$   
47:  $\delta_{\text{negupdate}} \gets \sum_{k} (m^{(2,k)} - m^{(2,k-1)})$  where it sums rank  $>1$  update in phase  $\ell$   
48: write  $m^{(3,\text{end})} = a^{\top}wa$  and  $\delta_{\text{negupdate}} = a^{\top}diag(\delta)a$   
49:  $m^{(3,\text{end}+1)} \gets m^{(3,\text{end})} + a^{\top}diag(\delta_{\text{sparse}})a$  where  $\delta_{\text{sparse}} = \delta 1_{|\delta| \geq \eta |w|}$ .  
50:  $m^{(3,\text{end}+1)} \gets m^{(3,\text{end})}$  with rows in  $i$  removed  
51: end if  
52:  $v \gets w + \delta_{\text{sparse}}$   
53: end  $\leftarrow$  end + 1  
54: end for  
55: ▷ step 4: add up the estimate change of leverage scores  
57:  $c \gets 0$   
58: for  $k = 1, \dots, t_3$  do  
59: write  $m^{(3,k)} = a^{\top}w^{(k)}a$  and  $m^{(3,k-1)} = a^{\top}w^{(k-1)}a$   
60: if there is no insert/delete between  $m^{(3,k)}$  and  $m^{(3,k-1)}$  then ▷ update  
61:  $w^{(k-\frac{1}{2})} \gets$  median  $(\frac{1}{2} w^{(k-1)}, w^{(k)}, 2w^{(k-1)})$  ▷ entry-wise median  
62:  $\delta_c \gets$  estimate of  $\sigma(w^{(k-\frac{1}{2})}) - \sigma(w^{(k-1)})$  using lemma 6.2 with error  $\frac{1}{n^{1000}}$   
63:  $\delta_c \gets \delta_c +$  estimate of  $\sigma(w^{(k)}) - \sigma(w^{(k-\frac{1}{2})})$  using lemma 6.2 with error  $\frac{1}{n^{1000}}$   
64: else ▷ insert/delete  
65:  $\delta_c \gets$  estimate of  $\sigma(w^{(k)}) - \sigma(w^{(k-1)})$  using lemma 6.2 with error  $\frac{1}{n^{1000}}$   
66: end if  
67:  $c \gets c + \delta_c$   
68: end for  
69: return  $v, c$   
70: endprocedure
```

# 7 simple deterministic leverage score maintenance

in this section, we give a simple deterministic leverage score maintenance data-structure which is used by both the inner and middle phases in our main data structure in section 5. as a subprocedure, we make use of the batched low-rank update procedure in section 6 which requires the following assumption for a sequence of updates acts:

assumption 7.1 (short sequence and small updates). let the sequence of matrices and vectors in acts be  $a^{(0)}, a^{(1)}, \dots, a^{(t)} \in \mathbb{r}^{m \times n}$  and  $w^{(0)}, w^{(1)}, \dots, w^{(t)} \in \mathbb{r}^m$ . we assume that  $t \leq n^{0.08}$ , the maximum number of rows is bounded by  $o(n)$ , and that the changes satisfy

- update:  $\| \log w^{(k)} - \log w^{(k - 1)} \|_2 \leq 0.01$ .  
- insert/delete:  $w_{a}aa^{\top}\preceq 0.01(a^{(k - 1)})^{\top}w^{(k - 1)}a^{(k - 1)}$

# 7.1 main result

theorem 7.2 (simple leverage score maintenance). given an initial matrix  $a \in \mathbb{r}^{m \times n}$  with  $m = o(n)$ , initial weight  $w \in \mathbb{r}_+^m$ , and error parameter  $\epsilon_{\mathrm{simp}} \leq 0.01$ . there is a deterministic data structure (algorithm 4) that approximately maintains the leverage scores

$$
\sigma_ {i} (w) = (\sqrt {w} a (a ^ {\top} w a) ^ {- 1} a ^ {\top} \sqrt {w}) _ {i, i},
$$

for positive diagonal matrices  $w = \mathrm{diag}(w)$  through the following operations:

1.  $\mathrm{init}(a,w,\epsilon_{\mathrm{simp}})$ : takes  $o(n^{\omega +o(1)})$  time to initialize the data structure.  
2. update(acts): update the matrix  $a$ , vector  $w$  and the approximation of the leverage scores for the sequence of updates in acts. moreover, if acts satisfies assumption 7.1, then the function update(acts) makes one call to pm.update² and takes extra time  $o(n^2 \cdot |\mathsf{acts}|)$  to compute a vector  $\delta \widetilde{\sigma} \in \mathbb{r}^m$  such that

$$
\| \delta \widetilde {\sigma} - \delta \sigma \| _ {2} \leq \frac {1}{n ^ {1 0 0}} + o \left(\epsilon_ {\text {s i m p}} + | \text {a c t s} | \cdot n ^ {- 0. 0 8}\right) \cdot | \text {a c t s} |.
$$

3. refineestimate  $(\widetilde{\sigma}^{\mathrm{new}})$ : takes  $o(n)$  time to update the approximation of  $\sigma(w)$  to  $\widetilde{\sigma}^{\mathrm{new}}$ .  
4. query(): takes  $o(n)$  time to output the approximation  $\widetilde{\sigma} \in \mathbb{r}^m$  maintained by the data structure.

proof of theorem 7.2. we only need to prove the guarantee for the function update(acts), as the guarantees for other functions are straightforward. notice that each call to the function update(acts) makes at most one call to pm.update, and the extra running time of  $o(n^{2} \cdot |\mathbf{acts}|)$  follows directly from theorem 6.3. to prove the error guarantee, we let the sequence of matrices and vectors in acts be  $a^{(0)}, a^{(1)}, \dots, a^{(t)}$  and  $w^{(0)}, w^{(1)}, \dots, w^{(t)}$ . notice that by theorem 6.3, we have

$$
\left\| \sigma_ {a ^ {(t)}} (w ^ {\mathrm {m i d}}) - \sigma_ {a ^ {(0)}} (w ^ {(0)}) - \delta \widetilde {\sigma} ^ {\mathrm {m i d}} \right\| _ {2} \leq \frac {1}{n ^ {1 0 0}}.
$$

it follows from lemma 7.6 that

$$
\left\| \sigma_ {a ^ {(t)}} (w ^ {(t)}) - \sigma_ {a ^ {(t)}} (w ^ {\mathrm {m i d}}) - \delta \widetilde {\sigma} ^ {\mathrm {n e w}} \right\| _ {2} \leq o (\epsilon_ {\mathrm {s i m p}} + | \mathsf {a c t s} | \cdot n ^ {- 0. 0 8}) \cdot | \mathsf {a c t s} |.
$$

the error guarantee then follows by summing up the two error upper bounds above.

the following corollary is an immediate consequence of theorem 7.2 and b.1. it states that if matrix multiplication can be performed fast enough, then the simple leverage score maintenance would imply a deterministic  $o(n^{3}\log (n / \epsilon))$  algorithm for the cutting plane method.

corollary 7.3. if  $\mathcal{t}_{\mathrm{mat}}(n,n,r) = o(n^{2}\log^{o(1)}(n))$  for  $r = n^{\alpha}$  with  $\alpha >2 / 3$ , then there's a deterministic  $o(n^{3}\log (n / \epsilon))$  time algorithm for the cutting plane method.

proof. this algorithm only uses the inner and middle phases. the inner phase is run as is our main algorithm. for the middle phase, we notice that, in this case, the exponent of matrix multiplication time can be bounded as  $\omega < 3 - 2/3 = 7/3$ . therefore, the data structure is restarted after every  $n^{1/3}$  calls to the middle phase. for the middle phase, we use the simple leverage score maintenance data structure in theorem 7.2 with parameter  $\epsilon_{\mathrm{simp}} = n^{-\alpha/2} / \log^{o(1)}(n)$ . it follows from theorem 7.2 that the error accumulated in the  $n^{0.2}$  steps is  $o(n^{-\alpha/2}n^{1/3}\log^{o(1)}(n)) = n^{-\omega(1)}$ . by theorem 7.2 and 5.1, the running time is  $o(n^2\log^{o(1)}(n))$  per step for middle phase which is fast enough. this finishes the proof of the corollary.

# 7.2 approximate leverage score's moving

lemma 7.4 (leverage score's moving). given any matrix  $a \in \mathbb{r}^{m \times n}$ , vectors  $w^{\mathrm{mid}}$ ,  $w^{\mathrm{new}} \in \mathbb{r}_+^m$ . the change in leverage score from  $w^{\mathrm{mid}}$  to  $w^{\mathrm{new}}$  can be written as

$$
\begin{array}{l} \delta \sigma_ {i} ^ {\mathrm {n e w}} := \sigma (w ^ {\mathrm {n e w}}) _ {i} - \sigma (w ^ {\mathrm {m i d}}) _ {i} \\ = (w _ {i} ^ {\mathrm {n e w}} - w _ {i} ^ {\mathrm {m i d}}) \cdot \tau (w ^ {\mathrm {m i d}}) _ {i} + w _ {i} ^ {\mathrm {n e w}} \cdot \int_ {0} ^ {1} e _ {i} ^ {\top} q (x _ {t}) (w ^ {\mathrm {m i d}} - w ^ {\mathrm {n e w}}) q (x _ {t}) e _ {i} \mathrm {d} t, \\ \end{array}
$$

where we define  $x_{t} = w^{\mathrm{mid}} + t(w^{\mathrm{new}} - w^{\mathrm{mid}})$  for each  $t\in [0,1]$ .

proof. we can rewrite  $\sigma(w^{\mathrm{new}})_i - \sigma(w^{\mathrm{mid}})_i$  as

$$
\sigma (w ^ {\mathrm {n e w}}) _ {i} - \sigma (w ^ {\mathrm {m i d}}) _ {i} = (w _ {i} ^ {\mathrm {n e w}} - w _ {i} ^ {\mathrm {m i d}}) \cdot \tau (w ^ {\mathrm {m i d}}) _ {i} + w _ {i} ^ {\mathrm {n e w}} (\tau (w ^ {\mathrm {n e w}}) _ {i} - \tau (w ^ {\mathrm {m i d}}) _ {i}).
$$

then the second term is computed as

$$
\begin{array}{l} w _ {i} ^ {\mathrm {n e w}} (\tau (w ^ {\mathrm {n e w}}) _ {i} - \tau (w ^ {\mathrm {m i d}}) _ {i}) \\ = w _ {i} ^ {\mathrm {n e w}} \cdot ((a (a ^ {\top} w ^ {\mathrm {n e w}} a) ^ {- 1} a ^ {\top}) _ {i, i} - (a (a ^ {\top} w ^ {\mathrm {m i d}} a) ^ {- 1} a ^ {\top}) _ {i, i}) \\ = w _ {i} ^ {\mathrm {n e w}} \cdot \int_ {0} ^ {1} e _ {i} ^ {\top} q (x _ {t}) \left(w ^ {\mathrm {m i d}} - w ^ {\mathrm {n e w}}\right) q (x _ {t}) e _ {i} \mathrm {d} t. \\ \end{array}
$$

where the last step follows by the definition of  $q(v) = a(a^{\top}va)^{-1}a^{\top}\in \mathbb{r}^{m\times m}$ .

lemma 7.5 (computing  $\delta \widetilde{\sigma}^{\mathrm{new}}$ ). given any matrix  $a \in \mathbb{r}^{m \times n}$ , vectors  $w^{\mathrm{mid}}$ ,  $w^{\mathrm{new}} \in \mathbb{r}_+^m$ , where  $m = o(n)$ . define an approximation  $\delta \widetilde{\sigma}_i^{\mathrm{new}}$  to the change in leverage score to be

$$
\delta \widetilde {\sigma} _ {i} ^ {\mathrm {n e w}} = (w _ {i} ^ {\mathrm {n e w}} - w _ {i} ^ {\mathrm {m i d}}) \cdot e _ {i} ^ {\top} q (v ^ {\mathrm {m i d}}) e _ {i} + w _ {i} ^ {\mathrm {n e w}} e _ {i} ^ {\top} q (v ^ {\mathrm {m i d}}) (w ^ {\mathrm {m i d}} - w ^ {\mathrm {n e w}}) q (v ^ {\mathrm {m i d}}) e _ {i}.
$$

then given the matrix  $q(v^{\mathrm{mid}})$ , we can compute  $\delta \widetilde{\sigma}_i^{\mathrm{new}}$  for all  $i \in [m]$  in time  $o(n^{2})$ .

algorithm 4  
1: data structure maintainleveragescoresimple  $\triangleright$  theorem 7.2   
2:   
3: members   
4:  $m\in \mathbb{z}_+$ $\triangleright m = \theta (n)$    
5:  $a\in \mathbb{r}^{m\times n}$    
6:  $w\in \mathbb{r}^m$ $\triangleright$  target vector   
7:  $\widetilde{\sigma}\in \mathbb{r}^m$ $\triangleright$  the approximate leverage scores   
8: projectionmaintain pm  $\triangleright$  theorem b.2 or b.4   
9: end members   
10:   
11: procedure init(  $a\in \mathbb{r}^{m\times n},w\in \mathbb{r}_{+}^{m},\epsilon_{\mathrm{simp}}\in \mathbb{r}_{+})$ $\triangleright$  initialization   
12:  $a\gets a,m\gets m,w\gets w$    
13:  $\widetilde{\sigma}\gets \sigma (w)$    
14: pm.init(a,w,epsilon)   
15: end procedure   
16:   
17: procedure update(actse vector<action>)  $\triangleright$  update estimate for a sequence of updates   
18: let  $a$  and  $w^{\mathrm{new}}$  be the final matrix and vector in acts   
19:  $w^{\mathrm{mid}},\delta \widetilde{\sigma}^{\mathrm{mid}}\gets$  batchedupdate(actse)  $\triangleright$  let  $w^{\mathrm{mid}},\delta \widetilde{\sigma}^{\mathrm{mid}}$  be computed as in theorem 6.3 (algorithm 2 and 3) with the sequence of updates in acts   
20:  $v^{\mathrm{mid}},m(v^{\mathrm{mid}})^{-1},q(v^{\mathrm{mid}})\gets$  pm.update(wmid)   
21: compute  $\delta \widetilde{\sigma}^{\mathrm{new}}$  as in lemma 7.5   
22:  $\widetilde{\sigma}\gets \widetilde{\sigma} +\delta \widetilde{\sigma}^{\mathrm{mid}} + \delta \widetilde{\sigma}^{\mathrm{new}}$    
23:  $w\gets w^{\mathrm{new}}$    
24: end procedure

26: procedure refineestimate(  $\tilde{\sigma}^{\mathrm{new}}\in \mathbb{r}_{+}^{m})$ $\triangleright$  refine the estimation  $\widetilde{\sigma}$  to  $\widetilde{\sigma}^{\mathrm{new}}$    
27:  $\widetilde{\sigma}\gets \widetilde{\sigma}^{\mathrm{new}}$    
28: end procedure   
29:

31: return  $\widetilde{\sigma}$    
32: end procedure   
33:   
34: end data structure

proof. since we know the matrix  $q(v^{\mathrm{mid}})$ , the first term is essentially reading all the diagonal entries of matrix  $q(v^{\mathrm{mid}})$ , which can be computed for all  $i \in [m]$  in time  $o(n)$ . for the second term, we denote  $q = q(x_{t})$  and  $q^{(2)}$  the entry-wise square of  $q$ . then we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} w _ {i} ^ {\text {n e w}} e _ {i} ^ {\top} q \left(w ^ {\text {m i d}} - w ^ {\text {n e w}}\right) q e _ {i} \\ = w _ {i} ^ {\mathrm {n e w}} \cdot \sum_ {j \in [ m ]} (q _ {i, j}) ^ {2} (w ^ {\mathrm {m i d}} - w ^ {\mathrm {n e w}}) _ {j} \\ = w _ {i} ^ {\text {n e w}} \cdot e _ {i} ^ {\top} q ^ {(2)} \left(w ^ {\text {m i d}} - w ^ {\text {n e w}}\right). \\ \end{array}
$$

therefore, in order to compute the second term for all  $i \in [m]$ , it suffices to compute the matrix-vector product  $q^{(2)}(w^{\mathrm{mid}} - w^{\mathrm{new}})$ , which takes time  $o(n^{2})$ .

# 7.3  $\ell_2$ -error bound

let  $a^{(t)}$  and  $a^{(0)}$  be the final and initial matrix in the sequence of updates in acts, and  $w^{(0)}$  be the initial vector in acts. denote  $\delta \sigma^{\mathrm{mid}} = \sigma_{a^{(t)}}(w^{\mathrm{mid}}) - \sigma_{a^{(0)}}(w^{(0)})$ . it follows from theorem 6.3 that if assumption 7.1 holds, then we have

$$
\left\| \delta \widetilde {\sigma} ^ {\text {m i d}} - \delta \sigma^ {\text {m i d}} \right\| _ {2} \leq \frac {1}{n ^ {1 0 0}}.
$$

it therefore suffices to bound the  $\ell_2$ -error  $\| \delta \widetilde{\sigma}^{\mathrm{new}} - \delta \sigma^{\mathrm{new}} \|_2$ . for simplicity, we use  $a$  to denote  $a^{(t)}$ . recall from lemma 7.4 that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \delta \sigma_ {i} ^ {\text {n e w}} := \sigma \left(w ^ {\text {n e w}}\right) _ {i} - \sigma \left(w ^ {\text {m i d}}\right) _ {i} \\ = \left(w _ {i} ^ {\text {n e w}} - w _ {i} ^ {\text {m i d}}\right) \cdot \tau \left(w ^ {\text {m i d}}\right) _ {i} + w _ {i} ^ {\text {n e w}} \cdot \int_ {0} ^ {1} e _ {i} ^ {\top} q \left(x _ {t}\right) \left(w ^ {\text {m i d}} - w ^ {\text {n e w}}\right) q \left(x _ {t}\right) e _ {i} \mathrm {d} t, \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $x_{t} = w^{\mathrm{mid}} + t(w^{\mathrm{new}} - w^{\mathrm{mid}})$  for each  $t\in [0,1]$ . also recall that in lemma 7.5, our approximation  $\delta \widetilde{\sigma}^{\mathrm{new}}$  is computed as

$$
\delta \widetilde {\sigma} _ {i} ^ {\mathrm {n e w}} = (w _ {i} ^ {\mathrm {n e w}} - w _ {i} ^ {\mathrm {m i d}}) \cdot e _ {i} ^ {\top} q (v ^ {\mathrm {m i d}}) e _ {i} + w _ {i} ^ {\mathrm {n e w}} e _ {i} ^ {\top} q (v ^ {\mathrm {m i d}}) (w ^ {\mathrm {m i d}} - w ^ {\mathrm {n e w}}) q (v ^ {\mathrm {m i d}}) e _ {i}.
$$

we use  $\delta_{1}$  and  $\delta_{2}$  to denote the two corresponding error terms

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left(\delta_ {1}\right) _ {i} = \left(w _ {i} ^ {\text {n e w}} - w _ {i} ^ {\text {m i d}}\right) \cdot e _ {i} ^ {\top} q \left(v ^ {\text {m i d}}\right) e _ {i} - \left(w _ {i} ^ {\text {n e w}} - w _ {i} ^ {\text {m i d}}\right) \cdot \tau \left(w ^ {\text {m i d}}\right) _ {i} \\ (\delta_ {2}) _ {i} = w _ {i} ^ {\mathrm {n e w}} e _ {i} ^ {\top} q (v ^ {\mathrm {m i d}}) (w ^ {\mathrm {m i d}} - w ^ {\mathrm {n e w}}) q (v ^ {\mathrm {m i d}}) e _ {i} - w _ {i} ^ {\mathrm {n e w}} \cdot \int_ {0} ^ {1} e _ {i} ^ {\top} q (x _ {t}) (w ^ {\mathrm {m i d}} - w ^ {\mathrm {n e w}}) q (x _ {t}) e _ {i} \mathrm {d} t, \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $x_{t} = w^{\mathrm{mid}} + t(w^{\mathrm{new}} - w^{\mathrm{mid}})$ . it follows that

$$
\left\| \delta \tilde {\sigma} ^ {\text {n e w}} - \delta \sigma^ {\text {n e w}} \right\| _ {2} \leq \left\| \delta_ {1} \right\| _ {2} + \left\| \delta_ {2} \right\| _ {2}.
$$

we prove the following bound on the  $\ell_2$ -error.

lemma 7.6 ( $\ell_2$ -error bound). assume assumption 7.1 holds and that  $\epsilon_{\mathrm{simp}} \leq 0.01$ , where  $\epsilon_{\mathrm{simp}}$  is the error parameter in algorithm 4. then the  $\ell_2$ -error is upper bounded as

$$
\left\| \delta \widetilde {\sigma} ^ {\text {n e w}} - \delta \sigma^ {\text {n e w}} \right\| _ {2} \leq o \left(\epsilon_ {\text {s i m p}} + | \text {a c t s} | \cdot n ^ {- 0. 0 8}\right) \cdot | \text {a c t s} |.
$$

proof. for simplicity, we assume here that  $w^{\mathrm{new}} - w^{\mathrm{mid}} \geq 0$  and that  $w^{\mathrm{mid}} - v^{\mathrm{mid}} \geq 0$ . this is without loss of generality, since in the case where these assumptions don't hold, we can break the corresponding terms into positive and negative parts and bound each one of them. the lemma then follows immediately from the following lemma 7.7 and 7.8.

lemma 7.7 ( $\| \delta_1\| _2$ ). assume assumption 7.1 holds and that  $\epsilon_{\mathrm{simp}}\leq 0.01$ . then we have the following upper bound on  $\| \delta_1\| _2$

$$
\left\| \delta_ {1} \right\| _ {2} \leq o \left(\epsilon_ {\mathrm {s i m p}}\right) \cdot | \mathsf {a c t s} |.
$$

proof. recall the definition of the error term  $\delta_{1}$  as

$$
(\delta_ {1}) _ {i} = (w _ {i} ^ {\mathrm {n e w}} - w _ {i} ^ {\mathrm {m i d}}) \cdot e _ {i} ^ {\top} q (v ^ {\mathrm {m i d}}) e _ {i} - (w _ {i} ^ {\mathrm {n e w}} - w _ {i} ^ {\mathrm {m i d}}) \cdot \tau (w ^ {\mathrm {m i d}}) _ {i}.
$$

we define  $y_{s} = w^{\mathrm{mid}} + s(v^{\mathrm{mid}} - w^{\mathrm{mid}})$  for  $s\in [0,1]$ . the term  $(\delta_1)_i$  can be rewritten as

$$
\begin{array}{l} (\delta_ {1}) _ {i} = (w _ {i} ^ {\mathrm {n e w}} - w _ {i} ^ {\mathrm {m i d}}) \cdot e _ {i} ^ {\top} q (v ^ {\mathrm {m i d}}) e _ {i} - (w _ {i} ^ {\mathrm {n e w}} - w _ {i} ^ {\mathrm {m i d}}) \cdot \tau (w ^ {\mathrm {m i d}}) _ {i} \\ = (w _ {i} ^ {\mathrm {n e w}} - w _ {i} ^ {\mathrm {m i d}}) \cdot e _ {i} ^ {\top} \left(\int_ {0} ^ {1} q (y _ {s}) (w ^ {\mathrm {m i d}} - v ^ {\mathrm {m i d}}) q (y _ {s}) \mathrm {d} s\right) e _ {i} \\ = e _ {i} ^ {\top} \sqrt {w ^ {\mathrm {n e w}} - w ^ {\mathrm {m i d}}} q (y _ {\xi}) (w ^ {\mathrm {m i d}} - v ^ {\mathrm {m i d}}) q (y _ {\xi}) \sqrt {w ^ {\mathrm {n e w}} - w ^ {\mathrm {m i d}}} e _ {i}, \\ \end{array}
$$

for some  $\xi \in [0,1]$ . it follows that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| \delta_ {1} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \leq \left\| \sqrt {\frac {w ^ {\mathrm {n e w}} - w ^ {\mathrm {m i d}}}{y _ {\xi}}} p (y _ {\xi}) \frac {w ^ {\mathrm {m i d}} - v ^ {\mathrm {m i d}}}{y _ {\xi}} p (y _ {\xi}) \sqrt {\frac {w ^ {\mathrm {n e w}} - w ^ {\mathrm {m i d}}}{y _ {\xi}}} \right\| _ {f} ^ {2} \\ = \operatorname {t r} [ (\sqrt {c} p b p \sqrt {c}) ^ {2} ], \\ \end{array}
$$

where in the last step we define diagonal matrices  $b, c \in \mathbb{r}^{m \times m}$  as

$$
b = \frac {w ^ {\mathrm {m i d}} - v ^ {\mathrm {m i d}}}{y _ {\xi}}, c = \frac {w ^ {\mathrm {n e w}} - w ^ {\mathrm {m i d}}}{y _ {\xi}},
$$

and we use  $p$  to denote  $p(y_{\xi})$ . then we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathrm {t r} [ (\sqrt {c} p b p \sqrt {c}) ^ {2} ] \\ \leq \| b \| _ {\infty} ^ {2} \cdot \operatorname {t r} [ c ^ {2} ] \quad p b p \preceq \| b \| _ {\infty} \cdot i \\ = o (1) \cdot \left\| \log (w ^ {\mathrm {m i d}}) - \log (v ^ {\mathrm {m i d}}) \right\| _ {\infty} ^ {2} \cdot \left\| \log (w ^ {\mathrm {n e w}}) - \log (w ^ {\mathrm {m i d}}) \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \\ \leq o \left(\epsilon_ {\text {s i m p}} ^ {2}\right) \cdot \left\| \log \left(w ^ {\text {n e w}}\right) - \log \left(w ^ {\text {m i d}}\right) \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \\ \leq o (\epsilon_ {\mathrm {s i m p}} ^ {2}) \cdot | \mathsf {a c t s} | ^ {2}, \\ \end{array}
$$

where the last step follows from theorem 6.3.

lemma 7.8 ( $\| \delta_2\| _2$ ). assume assumption 7.1 holds and that  $\epsilon_{\mathrm{simp}}\leq 0.01$ . then we have the following upper bound on  $\| \delta_2\| _2$

$$
\left\| \delta_ {2} \right\| _ {2} \leq o \left(\epsilon_ {\text {s i m p}} + | \text {a c t s} | \cdot n ^ {- 0. 0 8}\right) \cdot | \text {a c t s} |.
$$

proof. recall the definition of the error term  $\delta_2$  as

$$
(\delta_ {2}) _ {i} = w _ {i} ^ {\mathrm {n e w}} e _ {i} ^ {\top} q (v ^ {\mathrm {m i d}}) (w ^ {\mathrm {m i d}} - w ^ {\mathrm {n e w}}) q (v ^ {\mathrm {m i d}}) e _ {i} - w _ {i} ^ {\mathrm {n e w}} \cdot \int_ {0} ^ {1} e _ {i} ^ {\top} q (x _ {t}) (w ^ {\mathrm {m i d}} - w ^ {\mathrm {n e w}}) q (x _ {t}) e _ {i} \mathrm {d} t,
$$

where  $x_{t} = w^{\mathrm{mid}} + t(w^{\mathrm{new}} - w^{\mathrm{mid}})$ . it follows that there exists  $t \in [0,1]$  such that

$$
\begin{array}{l} (\delta_ {2}) _ {i} = w _ {i} ^ {\mathrm {n e w}} e _ {i} ^ {\top} q (v ^ {\mathrm {m i d}}) (w ^ {\mathrm {m i d}} - w ^ {\mathrm {n e w}}) q (v ^ {\mathrm {m i d}}) e _ {i} - w _ {i} ^ {\mathrm {n e w}} e _ {i} ^ {\top} q (x _ {t}) (w ^ {\mathrm {m i d}} - w ^ {\mathrm {n e w}}) q (x _ {t}) e _ {i} \\ = \int_ {0} ^ {1} \frac {\mathrm {d}}{\mathrm {d} s} \left(e _ {i} ^ {\top} \sqrt {w ^ {\mathrm {n e w}}} q (v _ {t, s}) (w ^ {\mathrm {m i d}} - w ^ {\mathrm {n e w}}) q (v _ {t, s}) \sqrt {w ^ {\mathrm {n e w}}} e _ {i}\right) \mathrm {d} s, \\ \end{array}
$$

where in the last line we define  $v_{t,s} = x_t + s(v^{\mathrm{mid}} - x_t)$  for  $s \in [0,1]$ . therefore, in order to bound the  $\| \delta_2\| _i$ , it suffices to bound the  $\ell_2$ -norm of the derivative:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| \delta_ {2} \| _ {2} \leq \int_ {0} ^ {1} \left\| \left(\frac {\mathrm {d}}{\mathrm {d} s} \left(e _ {i} ^ {\top} \sqrt {w ^ {\mathrm {n e w}}} q (v _ {t, s}) (w ^ {\mathrm {m i d}} - w ^ {\mathrm {n e w}}) q (v _ {t, s}) \sqrt {w ^ {\mathrm {n e w}}} e _ {i}\right)\right) _ {i = 1} ^ {m} \right\| _ {2} \mathrm {d} s \\ \leq \sup  _ {s \in [ 0, 1 ]} \left\| \left(\frac {\mathrm {d}}{\mathrm {d} s} \left(e _ {i} ^ {\top} \sqrt {w ^ {\mathrm {n e w}}} q (v _ {t, s}) (w ^ {\mathrm {m i d}} - w ^ {\mathrm {n e w}}) q (v _ {t, s}) \sqrt {w ^ {\mathrm {n e w}}} e _ {i}\right)\right) _ {i = 1} ^ {m} \right\| _ {2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

notice that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {\mathrm {d}}{\mathrm {d} s} \left(e _ {i} ^ {\top} \sqrt {w ^ {\text {n e w}}} q (v _ {t, s}) (w ^ {\text {m i d}} - w ^ {\text {n e w}}) q (v _ {t, s}) \sqrt {w ^ {\text {n e w}}} e _ {i}\right) \\ = - 2 e _ {i} ^ {\top} \sqrt {w ^ {\mathrm {n e w}}} q (v _ {t, s}) (v ^ {\mathrm {m i d}} - x _ {t}) q (v _ {t, s}) (w ^ {\mathrm {m i d}} - w ^ {\mathrm {n e w}}) q (v _ {t, s}) \sqrt {w ^ {\mathrm {n e w}}} e _ {i}. \\ \end{array}
$$

it follows that for any  $s \in [0,1]$ , we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \left(\frac {\mathrm {d}}{\mathrm {d} s} \left(e _ {i} ^ {\top} \sqrt {w ^ {\text {n e w}}} q (v _ {t, s}) \left(w ^ {\text {m i d}} - w ^ {\text {n e w}}\right) q (v _ {t, s}) \sqrt {w ^ {\text {n e w}}} e _ {i}\right)\right) _ {i = 1} ^ {m} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \\ \leq 4 \cdot \left\| \sqrt {w ^ {\text {n e w}}} q (v _ {t, s}) \left(v ^ {\text {m i d}} - x _ {t}\right) q (v _ {t, s}) \left(w ^ {\text {m i d}} - w ^ {\text {n e w}}\right) q (v _ {t, s}) \sqrt {w ^ {\text {n e w}}} \right\| _ {f} ^ {2} \\ = 4 \operatorname {t r} [ \sqrt {d} p c p b p d p b p c p \sqrt {d} ], \\ \end{array}
$$

where in the last step we define diagonal matrices  $b, c \in \mathbb{r}^{m \times m}$  as

$$
b = \frac {w ^ {\mathrm {m i d}} - w ^ {\mathrm {n e w}}}{v _ {t , s}}, c = \frac {v ^ {\mathrm {m i d}} - x _ {t}}{v _ {t , s}}, d = \frac {w ^ {\mathrm {n e w}}}{v _ {t , s}},
$$

and we use  $p$  to denote  $p(v_{t,s})$ . by assumption 7.1, we have

$$
\left\| \log (w ^ {\text {n e w}}) - \log (w ^ {\text {m i d}}) \right\| _ {\infty} \leq 0. 0 1.
$$

this together with the assumption that  $\epsilon_{\mathrm{simp}} \leq 0.01$  further implies that

$$
\left\| \log (w ^ {\text {n e w}}) - \log (v _ {s, t}) \right\| _ {\infty} \leq 0. 1.
$$

therefore, we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \operatorname {t r} [ \sqrt {d} p c p b p d p b p c p \sqrt {d} ] \\ \leq o (1) \cdot \operatorname {t r} [ \sqrt {d} p c p b ^ {2} p c p \sqrt {d} ] \quad p d p \preceq o (1) \cdot i \\ = o (1) \cdot \operatorname {t r} [ b p c p d p c p b ] \\ \leq o (1) \cdot \operatorname {t r} \left[ b p c ^ {2} p b \right] \quad p d p \preceq o (1) \cdot i \\ \leq o (1) \cdot \| c \| _ {\infty} ^ {2} \cdot \| b \| _ {2} ^ {2} \quad p c ^ {2} p \preceq \| c \| _ {\infty} ^ {2} \cdot i \\ \leq o (1) \cdot \left\| \log (v ^ {\mathrm {m i d}}) - \log (x _ {t}) \right\| _ {\infty} ^ {2} \cdot \| \log (w ^ {\mathrm {n e w}}) - \log (w) \| _ {2} ^ {2} \\ \leq o (1) \cdot \left(\epsilon_ {\mathrm {s i m p}} + \left\| \log (w ^ {\mathrm {n e w}}) - \log (w ^ {\mathrm {m i d}}) \right\| _ {\infty}\right) ^ {2} \cdot \| \log (w ^ {\mathrm {n e w}}) - \log (w) \| _ {2} ^ {2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

the lemma then follows from theorem 6.3 which gives  $\left\| \log (w^{\mathrm{new}}) - \log (w^{\mathrm{mid}})\right\|_{\infty}\leq |\mathsf{acts}|\cdot n^{-0.08}$  and  $\left\| \log (w^{\mathrm{new}}) - \log (w^{\mathrm{mid}})\right\| _2\leq 0.01|\mathsf{acts}|$ .

# 8 complicated randomized leverage score maintenance

in this section, we present a complicated randomized leverage score maintenance data structure which is used by the outer phase in our main data structure in section 5. we again make crucial use of the batched low-rank update procedure in section 6.

# 8.1 main result

theorem 8.1 (complicated leverage score maintenance). given an initial matrix  $a \in \mathbb{r}^{m \times n}$  with  $m = o(n)$ , initial weight  $w \in \mathbb{r}_+^m$ ,  $jl$  dimension  $r > 0$ , discrete sampling parameter  $n \in \mathbb{z}_+$ , and error parameter  $\epsilon_{\mathrm{comp}} < 0.01$ . there is a randomized data structure (algorithm 5) that approximately maintains the leverage scores

$$
\sigma_ {i} (w) = (\sqrt {w} a (a ^ {\top} w a) ^ {- 1} a ^ {\top} \sqrt {w}) _ {i, i},
$$

for positive diagonal matrices  $w = \mathrm{diag}(w)$  through the following operations:

1.  $\mathrm{init}(a,w,r,n,\epsilon_{\mathrm{comp}})$ : takes  $o(n^{\omega +o(1)})$  time to initialize the data structure.  
2. update(act): update the matrix  $a$ , vector  $w$  and the approximation of the leverage scores for the sequence of updates in acts. moreover, if acts satisfies assumption 7.1, then the function update(act) makes  $o(n)$  calls to pm.update and takes extra time  $o(n^{2} \cdot |\mathrm{acts}| + t_{\mathrm{mat}}(n,n,r) \cdot n^{3} \cdot \log(n))$  to compute a random vector  $\delta \widetilde{\sigma} \in \mathbb{r}^{m}$  with error upper bounded by

$$
\| \delta \sigma - \mathbf {e} [ \delta \widetilde {\sigma} ] \| _ {2} \leq \frac {1}{n ^ {1 0 0}} + o \left(\mathrm {p o l y} (n) / 2 ^ {2 n}\right),
$$

andvarianceupperboundedby

$$
\mathbf {e} [ \| \sigma (w ^ {\text {n e w}}) - \sigma (w) - \delta \widetilde {\sigma} \| _ {2} ^ {2} ] \leq o (n ^ {3} / r) \cdot \epsilon_ {\text {c o m p}} ^ {2} \cdot | \text {a c t s} | ^ {2}.
$$

3. refineestimate  $(\widetilde{\sigma}^{\mathrm{new}})$ : takes  $o(n)$  time to update the approximation of  $\sigma(w)$  to  $\widetilde{\sigma}^{\mathrm{new}}$ .  
4. query(): takes  $o(n)$  time to output the approximation  $\widetilde{\sigma} \in \mathbb{r}^m$  maintained by the data structure.

proof of theorem 8.1. we only need to prove the guarantee for the function  $\mathrm{update}(\mathbf{acts})$ , as the guarantees for other functions are straightforward. the running time upper bound for the function  $\mathrm{update}(\mathbf{acts})$  is given in lemma 8.7. the upper bound on the  $\ell_2$ -error  $\| \delta \sigma - \mathbf{e}[\delta \widetilde{\sigma}] \|_2$  follows from theorem 6.3 and the lemmas in section 8.6. the upper bound on the variance follows from the lemmas in section 8.4 and 8.5.

# 8.2 leverage score's moving

definition 8.2 ( $x_{t}, z_{t}$  and  $y_{s,t}$ ). given vectors  $w^{\mathrm{mid}}$ ,  $w^{\mathrm{new}} \in \mathbb{r}_{+}^{m}$  and  $v^{\mathrm{mid}} \in \mathbb{r}_{+}^{m}$ . for all  $t \in [0,1]$ , we define  $x_{t}$  to be  $w^{\mathrm{mid}} + t(w^{\mathrm{new}} - w^{\mathrm{mid}})$ .

to define  $z_{t}$  for  $t \in [0,1]$ , we first define  $z_{t}$  for all  $t \in \mathcal{t}$  as in algorithm 5. we then extend the definition of  $z_{t}$  to the entire interval [0,1] by connecting consecutive points with segments.

for any  $s \in [0,1]$ , we define  $y_{s,t} = z_t + s(x_t - z_t)$ .

algorithm 5  
1: data structure maintainleveragescorecomplicated  $\triangleright$  theorem 8.1   
2:   
3: members   
4:  $m\in \mathbb{z}_+$ $\triangleright m = \theta (n)$    
5:  $a\in \mathbb{r}^{m\times n}$    
6:  $\widetilde{\sigma}\in \mathbb{r}^m$ $\triangleright$  the approximate leverage scores   
7:  $w\in \mathbb{r}^m$ $\triangleright$  target vector   
8:  $r\in \mathbb{z}_{+}$ $\triangleright$  dimension of jl matrices   
9:  $n\in \mathbb{z}_{+},s,\mathcal{t}\subseteq [0,1]$ $\triangleright$  discrete sampling points   
10: projectionmaintain pm  $\triangleright$  theorem b.1   
11: end members   
12:   
13: procedure init(a  $\in \mathbb{r}^{m\times n}$ $w\in \mathbb{r}_+^m,r\in \mathbb{z}_+,n\in \mathbb{z}_+,e_{comp}\in r_+)$ $\triangleright$  initialization   
14:  $a\gets a,m\gets m,w\gets w,r\gets r,n\gets n,\widetilde{\sigma}\gets \sigma (w)$    
15: set s,t with size n and corresponding weights w as in theorem 3.10   
16: pm.init(a,w,epsilon)   
17: end procedure   
18:   
19: procedure update(acts  $\in$  vector<action>)  $\triangleright$  update estimate for a sequence of updates   
20: let a and  $w^{\mathrm{new}}$  be the final matrix and vector in acts   
21:  $w^{\mathrm{mid}},\delta \widetilde{\sigma}^{\mathrm{mid}}\leftarrow$  batchedupdate(acts)  $\triangleright$  let  $w^{\mathrm{mid}},\delta \widetilde{\sigma}^{\mathrm{mid}}$  be computed as in theorem 6.3 (algorithm 2, algorithm 3) with the sequence of updates in acts   
22:  $v^{\mathrm{mid}},m(v^{\mathrm{mid}})^{-1},q(v^{\mathrm{mid}})\leftarrow$  pm.update(wmid)   
23: for t in t do   
24:  $x_{t} = w^{\mathrm{mid}} + t\cdot (w^{\mathrm{new}} - w^{\mathrm{mid}})$ $\triangleright$ $w^{\mathrm{mid}} = x_0 = x_{\mathcal{t}_1}\to x_{\mathcal{t}_2}\to \dots \to x_{\mathcal{t}_{|\mathcal{t}|}} = w^{\mathrm{new}}$    
25:  $m(z_t)^{-1},q(z_t),z_t\gets$  pm.update(xt)  $\triangleright$ $v^{\mathrm{mid}} = z_0 = z_{\mathcal{t}_1}\rightarrow z_{\mathcal{t}_2}\rightarrow \dots \rightarrow z_{\mathcal{t}_{|\mathcal{t}|}} = v^{\mathrm{new}}$    
26: compute  $\theta_{i,t}^{\top}\theta_{i,t}$  for all i e [m] as in lemma 8.8   
27: for s in s do   
28:  $y_{s,t}\gets z_t + s\cdot (x_t - z_t)$    
29: sample independent  $r_{\alpha \beta ,s,t},r_{\eta ,s}\in \mathbb{r}^{r\times m}$  where each entry  $\sim \mathcal{n}(0,1 / r)$    
30: compute  $r_{\alpha \beta ,s,t}\alpha_{i,s,t},r_{\alpha \beta ,s,t}\beta_{i,s,t},r_{\eta ,s}\eta_{i,s}$  for all i e [m] as in lemma 8.9   
31: for s' in s do   
32: sample  $r_{\gamma ,s,s',t}\in \mathbb{r}^{r\times m}$  where each entry  $\sim \mathcal{n}(0,1 / r)$    
33: compute  $r_{\gamma ,s,s',t}\gamma_{i,s,t}$  and  $r_{\gamma ,s,s',t}\gamma_{i,s',t}$  for all i e [m] as in lemma 8.9   
34: end for   
35: end for   
36: end for   
37: compute  $\delta \widetilde{\sigma}^{\mathrm{new}}$  as in lemma 8.10   
38:  $\widetilde{\sigma}\gets \widetilde{\sigma} +\delta \widetilde{\sigma}^{\mathrm{mid}} + \delta \widetilde{\sigma}^{\mathrm{new}},w\gets w^{\mathrm{new}}$    
39: end procedure   
40:   
41: procedure refineestimate(  $\widetilde{\sigma}^{\mathrm{new}}\in \mathbb{r}_{+}^{m})$ $\triangleright$  refine the estimation  $\widetilde{\sigma}$  to  $\widetilde{\sigma}^{\mathrm{new}}$    
42:  $\widetilde{\sigma}\gets \widetilde{\sigma}^{\mathrm{new}}$    
43: end procedure   
44:   
45: procedure query()   
46: return  $\widetilde{\sigma}$    
47: end procedure   
48: 47   
49: end data structure
